Stage 0 to stage 1: automation becomes dispatch
The causal sequence begins with a human principal assigning work to an agent. A control plane then decomposes work into
planning, retrieval, reasoning, generation, validation, repair, and escalation. It assigns models, tools, context, compute, and
verification policies to each step. This can be economically valuable because it substitutes a portfolio policy for one static model.
Nothing in this sequence creates a market. Demand and supply remain under one residual claimant. The inventory is internal
compute and attention. The allocation rule is a policy score rather than a bid. Payment is payroll, cloud billing, or internal transfer
pricing. Failure is handled through retry or escalation rather than counterparty default. The right comparison is make-versus-buy
and centralized versus decentralized control, not market versus no market.

SCENARIO DOSSIER / NON-PRESCRIPTIVE

139

ODYLITH / MARKET EVOLUTION SITUATIONAL AWARENESS

EVIDENCE CUTOFF: 5 AUGUST 2026

An internal chargeback does not change the result if the enterprise can administratively rewrite the price, mandate the supplier,
absorb every loss, and prevent external entry. It is a shadow price used for management. It becomes market-facing only when an
authorized buyer agent can reject internal supply and contract with an independent seller under enforceable terms.

Stage 1 to stage 2: dispatch becomes a bundled platform
The platform can combine frontier models, open models, clouds, specialist tools, verifiers, and humans. The customer receives
one contract and one outcome surface. The platform buys or licenses the inputs and may switch them without the customer's
involvement.
This is vertical production. The platform is a reseller, systems integrator, or product firm. It can create upstream procurement
demand, but the customer is not participating in a market among the component suppliers. The platform owns the allocation rule,
sees private supplier data, sets the retail price, and normally bears or disclaims the customer-facing obligation. High usage can
coexist with zero two-sided liquidity.
The distinction matters because a platform can have strong bargaining power without operating a marketplace. It may use
suppliers as inputs, acquire successful specialists, reserve favored latency for its own services, and hide routing rebates. Market
language can obscure the fact that the customer's choice is only whether to buy the bundle.

Stage 2 to stage 3: interoperability becomes discovery
Protocols reduce the cost of describing capabilities, delegating tasks, authenticating actors, and conveying authority. A2A's April
2025 design includes Agent Cards, task lifecycles, artifacts, and messages. The Linux Foundation became its neutral home in
June 2025 ([Google A2A, 9 April 2025](https://developers.googleblog.com/en/a2a-a-new-era-of-agent-interope…), [Linux
Foundation, 23 June 2025](https://www.linuxfoundation.org/press/linux-foundation-launches-the-…)).
MCP's 18 June 2025 authorization specification binds access tokens to intended resources and forbids token passthrough,
addressing confused-deputy risk. IETF RFC 8693, published in January 2020, distinguishes delegation from impersonation and
supports actor chains while leaving the trust model and local policy out of scope. W3C Verifiable Credentials 2.0 became a
Recommendation on 15 May 2025 and supports cryptographically verifiable claims among issuers, holders, and verifiers ([MCP
authorization, 18 June 2025](https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2025-06-18/basic/autho…), [RFC
8693](https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/rfc8693/), [W3C VC 2.0 history](https://www.w3.org/standards/history/vc-data-model/)).
These facts lower coordination costs. They do not create a price, contract, liability allocation, settlement rule, or remedy. An
Agent Card is closer to a machine-readable business card than an executable ask. A credential proves that an issuer made a
claim; it does not prove that the holder is solvent, licensed for the transaction, legally bound, or capable of satisfying a judgment.
Discovery becomes a market only after offers, authority, delivery, payment, and disputes are integrated.

Stage 3 to stage 4: discovery becomes procurement
The first plausible market transition occurs when an enterprise buyer agent has a funded mandate and can issue a structured
RFQ to several independent suppliers. The buyer specifies the task, security boundary, delivery date, acceptance contract,
liability requirements, and budget. Suppliers return executable quotes. The buyer can reject all offers. An award creates a
contract, delivery is verified, and payment settles under existing procurement rails.
This is a real market even if only three suppliers bid, contracts settle on net-30 terms, and a human contracting officer approves
the award. Human involvement does not negate a market. It negates only a strong claim of fully autonomous machine commerce.
The relevant liquidity measures are qualified response count, time to quote, completion rate, repeat participation, and
ownership-adjusted concentration.
An approved-vendor network without repeated competition is not enough. It may be a procurement club or a collection of bilateral
relationships. A buyer that always awards to the incumbent under a master contract has automated purchasing, not market
formation.

SCENARIO DOSSIER / NON-PRESCRIPTIVE

140

ODYLITH / MARKET EVOLUTION SITUATIONAL AWARENESS

EVIDENCE CUTOFF: 5 AUGUST 2026

Stage 4 to stage 5: procurement becomes auction
A reverse auction can compress price discovery when requirements are sufficiently clear and several suppliers can satisfy them.
Current U.S. federal acquisition rules state that reverse auctions may be appropriate only when market research indicates a
competitive marketplace, multiple offerors can satisfy the requirement, and the specification encourages iterative bidding. The
rules also bar a reverse-auction service provider and conflicted affiliates from bidding in the auction it hosts ([FAR Subpart 17.8,
effective 13 March 2026](https://login.acquisition.gov/far/subpart-17.8)).
Those conditions expose the difference between an auction and dispatch. In dispatch, the controller observes or predicts costs
and assigns a task. Suppliers do not choose bids, withhold capacity, or bear an award obligation. In an auction, suppliers
strategically reveal information under a rule, can decline to bid, and bear consequences if they win.
Reverse auctions are fragile for AI services because quality is multidimensional and partially hidden. A lowest-price rule invites
compute shading, under-scoped verification, strategic latency promises, or renegotiation after lock-in. Bidders that use the same
foundation model, cloud, or pricing algorithm may appear independent while sharing common cost and failure shocks. Repeated
auctions with transparent low bids can also make tacit coordination easier. In March 2024, the FTC and DOJ stated that
competitors' joint reliance on shared pricing recommendations or algorithms can support unlawful coordination even when they
retain some pricing discretion ([FTC and DOJ, 28 March
2024](https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/news/press-releases/2024/03/ftc-doj-…)).
An auction therefore passes only when the lot is comparable, technical eligibility is meaningful, bidder independence is adjusted
for ownership and dependencies, the host is neutral, post-award quality is observable, and default has a remedy. The existence
of an auction interface proves none of these conditions by itself.

Stage 5 to stage 6: auctions become spot exchange
A spot market becomes possible when the object is fungible enough for repeated machine ordering. Examples include an
authenticated API call, a bounded tool invocation, a fixed data entitlement, a token-throughput block, a latency-region class, or a
standard verification service. Sellers publish executable prices or asks. Buyers submit bids or accept posted offers. Metering
identifies delivery. Payment is authorized and settlement reaches defined finality.
Google's AP2 announcement of 16 September 2025 describes signed intent and cart mandates intended to establish authority,
authenticity, and accountability across cards, transfers, and stablecoins. It is explicitly payment-agnostic and connects to existing
payment methods ([Google AP2, 16 September
2025](https://cloud.google.com/blog/products/ai-machine-learning/announcin…)). That makes AP2 an order-authority and
evidence layer, not a final settlement rail. The distinction is material: a valid mandate can coexist with a failed bank transfer,
chargeback, sanctions block, merchant default, or disputed delivery.
Spot exchange requires more than a protocol path. Inventory must be reservable, offers must be executable, metering must be
attributable, and a legal person must stand behind each side. Liquidity must be demonstrated by fills, depth, spreads, slippage,
and repeat cohorts after removing subsidies, retries, internal trades, and wash volume.

Stage 6 to stage 7: spot calls become heterogeneous outcomes
Complex work does not become fungible merely because agents perform it. A security review, software migration, scientific
analysis, or regulatory filing contains hidden context and state-dependent quality. The relevant institution is closer to a
professional-services market than a token exchange.
The seller is a legal operator, firm, or human-agent team. It quotes price, deadline, method constraints, success probability, and
warranty. Inventory is expert time, domain access, or project capacity. Verification occurs through milestones, tests, proofs, and
independent expert judgment. Escrow and holdbacks reduce payment risk. Reputation must be conditioned on task difficulty and
buyer behavior.
This can be a genuine market without autonomous legal personhood for the software agent. The agent is an instrument of a
principal. The core principal-agent problem remains: the human or firm may not observe the agent's effort, while the buyer may
not observe the operator's model, subcontractors, or hidden retries. Outcome payment can align incentives, but it can also
intensify metric gaming and rejection of valuable hard cases.

SCENARIO DOSSIER / NON-PRESCRIPTIVE

141

ODYLITH / MARKET EVOLUTION SITUATIONAL AWARENESS

EVIDENCE CUTOFF: 5 AUGUST 2026

Stage 7 to stage 8: performance contracts become assurance and underwriting
Verification, audit, warranty, and insurance are separate economic services. A verifier sells information or evidence. An auditor
sells an independent process opinion. A warranty provider promises a remedy. An insurer accepts a defined risk in return for
premium, subject to limits, exclusions, reserves, capital, and claims adjudication.
These roles are not interchangeable. Perfect replay of a software trace can establish what happened without establishing
negligence, legal causation, economic damages, or whether the event is covered. A success-probability estimate is not risk
transfer. An SLA credit may be a service remedy rather than insurance. The legal classification depends on the obligation and
jurisdiction.
The assurance market is especially vulnerable to capture. If the router selects the supplier, defines the metric, verifies the
outcome, controls the reputation record, prices the guarantee, and adjudicates the claim, it can choose easy risks, suppress
negative evidence, or redefine success after the fact. The apparent efficiency of integration removes independent checks. The
market-formation test therefore treats verifier independence, loss-bearing solvency, claims rules, and appeal as first-order
conditions.

Stage 8 to stage 9: assurance becomes clearing
Clearing is the last stage because it manages obligations created elsewhere. A clearing layer can net positions, require collateral,
coordinate delivery-versus-payment, define finality, and handle member default. It cannot create genuine demand, independent
supply, comparable inventory, or competitive orders.
The BIS/CPMI-IOSCO Principles for Financial Market Infrastructures require clear settlement finality, controlled
money-settlement risk, adequate liquid resources, collateral and margin where relevant, participant-default rules, and public
procedures. These obligations survive automation ([PFMI, 16 April 2012](https://www.bis.org/cpmi/publ/d101.htm), [PFMI
principle index](https://www.bis.org/pfmi/help/principleid.htm)).
A premature clearinghouse can create a liquidity illusion. It can process internal platform obligations, testnet transfers, or one
anchor buyer's procurements and report gross volume. If the underlying orders are non-competitive, reversible, subsidized, or
controlled by one principal, central clearing adds operational and credit concentration without creating a market.

3. Markets versus platforms and hierarchies
The platform boundary is determined by who contracts with whom and who bears residual risk.
In a hierarchy, the controller can direct supply, change internal prices, absorb losses, and bar entry. In a bundled platform, the
customer contracts with the platform, while the platform privately buys inputs. In a marketplace, independent sellers make
executable offers to buyers and retain delivery and liability obligations under a venue rulebook. An institution can operate all three
forms in different layers.
Platforms have structural incentives to collapse markets back into hierarchy. They can bundle first-party agents at a zero
apparent price financed by cloud or SaaS revenue, reserve better latency and data for their own services, pay hidden routing
rebates, tie agent approval to proprietary identity and payment, retain reputation, acquire successful suppliers, and make
nominally open protocols depend on proprietary extensions.
DOJ's 2023 Merger Guidelines explicitly distinguish competition between platforms, competition on a platform, and competition to
displace one. They also identify the conflict that arises when the operator competes as a participant ([DOJ Guideline 9, 18
December 2023](https://www.justice.gov/atr/merger-guidelines/applying-merger-guidel…)). The EU Digital Markets Act supplies
concrete institutional counterfactuals through anti-steering, direct contracting, restrictions on tying identification or payment
services, switching rights, third-party installation, and the prohibition on favoring a gatekeeper's own products in ranking
([Regulation (EU) 2022/1925, 14 September 2022](https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/reg/2022/1925/2022-10-12/eng)).
Cloud infrastructure demonstrates why technical multi-homing is not enough. The UK CMA's final cloud investigation published
on 31 July 2025 examined egress fees, interoperability, committed spend, licensing, switching, and multi-cloud, and identified
significant market power concerns. A buyer may have many provider logos yet face high commercial switching costs ([CMA cloud
market investigation](https://www.gov.uk/cma-cases/cloud-services-market-investigation)).

SCENARIO DOSSIER / NON-PRESCRIPTIVE

142

ODYLITH / MARKET EVOLUTION SITUATIONAL AWARENESS

EVIDENCE CUTOFF: 5 AUGUST 2026

4. Routing versus run-all-and-select
Routing chooses a supplier or execution policy before observing every possible result. It is valuable when execution has
non-trivial cost, latency, privacy exposure, rights constraints, or opportunity cost. The router estimates expected utility and
commits resources selectively.
Run-all-and-select executes several suppliers on the same task and chooses after observing outputs. It can reduce ex-ante
adverse selection because the buyer sees realized candidates. It also changes the game:
1. If every supplier is paid its posted execution price, the institution is a buyer-funded bake-off. There is no competitive bid and
no supplier-side market allocation.
2. If only the winner is paid, it is a tournament. Suppliers bear speculative compute and may decline hard or expensive tasks.
3. If suppliers bid price and quality terms before execution and an enforceable rule awards the contract, it becomes a
procurement mechanism.
4. If the evaluator is known, suppliers optimize toward it, creating benchmark leakage and Goodhart effects.
5. If outputs reveal proprietary methods or data, running all candidates expands rights and confidentiality risk.
6. If nominally different suppliers share a model or cloud, the extra runs add cost without adding meaningful diversity.
The run-all counterfactual is useful because it reveals what routing value actually consists of. If verification is perfect and free but
execution remains costly, routing retains value as cost and capacity allocation. If execution is also free, instantaneous, private,
and rights-neutral, run-all can dominate ex-ante model selection. The resulting problem is no longer which model call to buy. It is
who owns the outputs, how attention is allocated, and how suppliers are rewarded.

5. Perfect and free verification
Perfect verification eliminates important frictions but not the market institution.
It removes or weakens output-quality adverse selection, many forms of hidden action, superficial certification, quality reputation,
basic audit demand, and part of the risk premium. It makes pay-for-outcome and run-all mechanisms easier. It can commoditize
suppliers whose only differentiation was unverifiable quality.
It does not establish authority, identity, legal liability, solvency, privacy, data rights, IP ownership, scarce compute, latency,
availability, payment finality, tax, licensing, sanctions, switching, or remedies. It does not prevent collusion, self-preference,
foreclosure, or a common cloud outage. It does not tell a court whether a technically verified output caused a legally
compensable loss.
An assurance market for basic quality would contract under perfect verification. Insurance could remain for operational, legal, and
systemic loss, but premiums would reflect residual risk rather than uncertainty about the immediate output. Reputation would
retain value for availability, payment behavior, dispute conduct, and future capacity even if output quality were perfectly
observable.
The falsifier is strong: if a claimed market disappears when verification becomes cheap, its scarce product may have been
opacity or certification rent rather than the underlying service.

6. Supplier consolidation
Supplier count must be adjusted for ownership and dependency. Five agent brands running on one frontier model and one cloud
are not five independent failure domains. Three bidders using the same router pricing service are not fully independent price
setters.
Consolidation changes each mechanism:
• Internal control planes remain viable because they are not markets.
• Bundled platforms gain bargaining and foreclosure power.
• Directories retain many listings but lose substitutability.
• Procurement markets become oligopsony facing oligopoly.

SCENARIO DOSSIER / NON-PRESCRIPTIVE

143

ODYLITH / MARKET EVOLUTION SITUATIONAL AWARENESS

EVIDENCE CUTOFF: 5 AUGUST 2026

• Reverse auctions become hollow or facilitate signaling among repeated bidders.
• Spot exchanges become distribution channels for a few wholesalers.
• Specialist niches may survive where domain assets are local.
• Insurers face greater correlated tail exposure and lower diversification.
• Clearing concentrates common operational and credit risk.
A market can exist under oligopoly, but the claims of liquidity, robust price discovery, and resilience become weaker. The
decisive measures are parent ownership, common models, common clouds, common training data, common verifiers, and
shared pricing or routing software.

7. Vertical trust conflict
Combining routing, verification, reputation, insurance, and adjudication creates a closed trust loop.
The router can favor suppliers that pay rebates. The verifier can define success to validate the router's choices. The reputation
system can suppress failed or disputed tasks. The insurer can exclude tasks predicted to lose. The adjudicator can interpret
ambiguous evidence in favor of the integrated platform. Each function then confirms the others.
The integrated operator's payoff can be written as:
platform profit = usage fees + routing rebates + data rents + premiums - execution cost - paid
claims - regulatory cost
The buyer's payoff is:
buyer utility = outcome value - price - monitoring cost - expected uncovered loss - switching
cost
These objectives diverge whenever the platform can increase fees or reduce paid claims without increasing buyer value.
Integration can reduce transaction cost, but it also removes independent contradiction. The formation test therefore asks whether
evidence is portable, metrics are fixed before execution, routing objectives and rebates are disclosed, reputation includes
denominators and disputes, risk capital is segregated, and claims can reach a neutral forum.

8. Liquidity illusions
The following quantities are not liquidity by themselves:
• agent cards or catalog listings;
• API calls, especially retries;
• internal routing decisions;
• gross payment messages before reversals;
• downloads or GitHub stars;
• reserved capacity listed on several venues;
• transactions funded by platform credits;
• trade among entities under common control;
• one anchor buyer's procurement volume;
• imputed prices inside a bundle;
• testnet or sandbox activity;
• insurer quote volume without bound coverage.
Evidence of liquidity requires ownership-adjusted executable depth, actual fills, repeat buyers and sellers, spread and slippage,
time to match, unsubsidized retention, low bypass leakage, and resilience when a major supplier or buyer exits. A thin market
can still be real, but it must be labeled thin.

SCENARIO DOSSIER / NON-PRESCRIPTIVE

144

ODYLITH / MARKET EVOLUTION SITUATIONAL AWARENESS

EVIDENCE CUTOFF: 5 AUGUST 2026

9. Opposite worlds
World A: genuine machine market
An enterprise issues a signed and scoped purchasing mandate. Several ownership-independent suppliers multi-home across
venues. A task has a machine-readable acceptance contract. Suppliers submit executable price-quality offers and bond
performance. A neutral verifier checks delivery. Escrow or clearing links delivery and payment. Portable history follows each
participant. An independent insurer prices residual risk. Defaults and appeals follow a public rulebook. Entry, congestion,
capacity shocks, and demand changes measurably affect prices and allocation.

World B: hierarchy with market vocabulary
An enterprise control plane chooses among API names under one or two master contracts. The suppliers share the same model
and cloud. Prices are catalog rates or internal imputed costs. The platform controls routing, verification, reputation, and claims. Its
remedy is a service credit while the enterprise bears most consequential loss. Billing settles monthly between enterprise and
platform. A marketplace page contains listings but no executable offers. Agent traffic grows quickly without decentralized
exchange.

World C: machine-assisted procurement
Buyer agents automate RFQs and bid comparison among approved suppliers. Contracts and remedies are real, but humans
approve material awards, tasks remain heterogeneous, liquidity is episodic, and settlement uses existing invoice and bank rails.
This is a genuine procurement market but not autonomous machine clearing.

World D: verification abundance without supplier markets
Outputs are perfectly and cheaply verified. Enterprises run several internal and bundled models, select the best result, and pay
one platform subscription. Quality uncertainty falls, but independent suppliers do not price or contract directly. Verification
improves hierarchy rather than creating a market.

World E: consolidated machine commerce
Two cloud-platform-payment groups own most models, agent stores, identity approval, reputation, and settlement. Agents
transact extensively inside each group. Cross-group interoperability is costly and reputation does not port. There are large
commercial networks and perhaps competition between ecosystems, but limited competition on each platform and no neutral
clearing market.

10. Hidden-confounder attacks
1. Internal-GMV attack. Internal chargebacks are counted as external transactions.
2. Human-shadow attack. A human selects every supposedly autonomous purchase.
3. Master-contract attack. Per-call routing occurs entirely inside one annual supplier contract.
4. Common-parent attack. Several brands share ownership.
5. Common-stack attack. Nominal competitors share model, cloud, data, verifier, or code.
6. Demo-money attack. Testnet, sandbox, credits, or unreconciled messages are called settlement.
7. Subsidized-liquidity attack. Incentives fund both sides and activity vanishes when credits end.
8. Retry-demand attack. Failures and retries are counted as new demand.
9. Listing-supply attack. Catalog entries are counted as active inventory.
10. Imputed-price attack. A bundled subscription is allocated to calls and presented as a market price.
11. Capacity-double-count attack. One capacity block is listed simultaneously on several venues.
12. Verifier-ownership attack. Seller, platform, and verifier share economics.
13. Reputation-wash attack. Sybils, reciprocal activity, or selective reporting inflate trust.
14. Hidden-rebate attack. A router appears neutral while suppliers pay for allocation.

SCENARIO DOSSIER / NON-PRESCRIPTIVE

145

ODYLITH / MARKET EVOLUTION SITUATIONAL AWARENESS

EVIDENCE CUTOFF: 5 AUGUST 2026

15. Benchmark-leakage attack. Suppliers train on the evaluator and fail out of sample.
16. Anchor-buyer attack. One sponsor creates almost all demand.
17. Reversibility attack. Gross payment ignores refunds, chargebacks, and failed finality.
18. Selection-denominator attack. Only successful or easily verified tasks enter performance history.
19. Jurisdiction attack. Protocol interoperability hides tax, insurance, employment, sanctions, or licensing barriers.
20. Goodhart attack. The success metric becomes the target and reduces buyer value.
21. Bypass-selection attack. Strong buyers and sellers leave after discovery, leaving adverse risk on-platform.
22. Dispute-suppression attack. Contested trades are removed from public performance data.
23. Supplier-financing attack. Platform loans or guarantees make nominal sellers dependent on the venue.
24. Latency-tier attack. First-party agents receive hidden priority, making third-party quality appear lower.

11. Indicators and falsifiers
Leading indicators of genuine formation
• Ownership-adjusted unique active buyers and sellers.
• Median number of independent qualified bids per task.
• Executable fill rate, depth, spread, slippage, and time to match.
• Repeat participation after subsidies expire.
• Share of purchases made under binding delegated mandates without human supplier selection.
• External settled value net of refunds, wash activity, internal transfers, retries, and credits.
• Multi-homing and successful switching rates.
• Direct bypass rates after first match.
• First-party versus third-party allocation after quality and risk adjustment.
• Portability and actual reuse of reputation across venues.
• Dependency concentration by model, cloud, data, verifier, and pricing algorithm.
• Default, dispute, refund, and claim-payment rates.
• Time to resolve disputes and percentage reaching neutral review.
• Verifier disagreement, calibration, and revenue independence.
• Underwriter limits, exclusions, capital, reserves, reinsurance, and paid claims.
• Price and allocation response to entry, congestion, supply outages, and demand shocks.

Falsifiers
• No external legal counterparty or money settlement.
• All transactions are internal scheduler decisions.
• Catalogs have no executable orders.
• Almost all supply shares one parent or common dependency.
• Prices are administrative allocations inside bundles.
• Agents cannot bind principals and humans reconstruct each contract.
• Payment protocols generate messages but no final settled value.
• Underwriting consists only of small SLA credits with no material risk transfer.
• Verifier and underwriter are the same interested party with no independent appeal.
• Activity collapses when credits end.
• Reputation cannot leave the platform.
• There is no remedy beyond delisting.

SCENARIO DOSSIER / NON-PRESCRIPTIVE

146

ODYLITH / MARKET EVOLUTION SITUATIONAL AWARENESS

EVIDENCE CUTOFF: 5 AUGUST 2026

• Technical switching exists but commercial switching does not.
• Gross activity grows without growth in competitive external purchases.
• A clearing layer launches before repeated underlying competitive trades exist.
• Perfect verification eliminates the purported market rather than improving its allocation.

Conclusion
The rigorous dividing line is institutional, not technological. AI agents can automate hierarchy, procurement, marketplaces, and
clearing alike. Autonomy does not choose among them.
The most plausible early external markets are thin procurement mechanisms: structured RFQs, reverse auctions for standardized
lots, posted-price API exchange, specialist outcome contracts, and compute-capacity trades. Directories and protocols lower
search and authorization costs but do not themselves create markets. Bundled platforms can generate large revenue while
internalizing supplier choice. Clearing is meaningful only after underlying competitive obligations exist. Outcome underwriting is a
separate risk-transfer institution whose formation depends on independent verification, real loss-bearing capital, correlated-risk
control, claims governance, and legal enforceability.
The opposite world remains fully coherent: agent activity can expand by orders of magnitude while most economic decisions stay
inside enterprises and vertically integrated platforms. Any claim of market formation is therefore falsifiable only through
counterparty independence, executable allocation, external settlement, measured liquidity, portable reputation, default
experience, and neutral dispute resolution.

SCENARIO DOSSIER / NON-PRESCRIPTIVE

ODYLITH / MARKET EVOLUTION SITUATIONAL AWARENESS

EVIDENCE CUTOFF: 5 AUGUST 2026

Round 2 cross-examination: antifragility and
falsification
Date of evidence review: 2026-08-05

Scope and conclusion boundary
This memo asks a narrow existential question: can any durable independent economic position exist around AI routing, demand,
context, verification, authorization, governance, evidence, reputation, procurement, settlement, insurance, or trust? It does not
ask whether those functions are useful. Useful functions are routinely absorbed into cloud platforms, identity suites, payment
networks, open standards, consultancies, and regulated institutions. Durability requires a scarce control point, a buyer with an
enduring reason to pay, and a way to retain value when models, protocols, and incumbent bundles improve.
The strongest falsification result is that a coherent death world exists. If intelligence becomes cheap and reliable, agent protocols
converge, customers keep evidence and policy in their own clouds, major platforms bundle routing and governance, payment
networks own commercial identity and settlement, accredited bodies own conformity, and insurers retain risk capital and loss
data, then the independent layer has no necessary control point. It may remain useful software, but useful software is not the
same claim as a durable company.
No scarcity examined here survives unconditionally. The best survivors revive only under explicit opposite-world conditions:
persistent multi-provider fragmentation; legally meaningful evidence whose custody cannot be delegated to a conflicted platform;
customer demand for provider-neutral policy enforcement; accreditation or statutory recognition; physical-world inspection; or
balance-sheet risk transfer. These are conditions, not recommendations.

Definitions that prevent category errors
The word "antifragile" is often used to relabel ordinary robustness. This memo uses stricter tests.
• Resistance: performance changes little under a bounded shock. Redundant providers can resist one provider outage.
• Resilience: performance falls and then recovers. A replayable workflow can restore service after corruption.
• Bargaining power: a party captures more of the value created or can credibly exit a dependency. Portability can improve
bargaining power without improving the underlying system.
• Learning: observations improve a model or rule set. Learning is not antifragility if the cost of gathering failures exceeds the
gain, if rights to the data are absent, or if attackers learn faster.
• Antifragility: the mechanism gains a measurable capability, asset, or option because a specified class of shocks occurred,
with bounded downside and an identifiable positive-convexity region. More volatility alone is not a benefit.
• Positive convexity: the upside from additional shock intensity rises faster than the downside over a defined interval. An
incident corpus can be locally convex if each new incident improves reusable detection across many deployments while
losses remain capped. It becomes concave when rare events are correlated, labels are wrong, or remediation costs scale
faster than reuse.
• Survival: remaining solvent and operational. Survival is a precondition, not proof of bargaining power or antifragility.
• A real option: a paid, time-bounded right but not an obligation to expand, switch, enter, or abandon after information arrives. A
backlog item with no exercise price, expiration, owner, or abandonment rule is not an option.
The test for every mechanism is therefore: shock -> protected downside -> captured learning or exercise right -> measurable gain
-> retained rights -> bounded failure. If any arrow is missing, the mechanism is resistance, resilience, bargaining power, or
narrative optionality rather than antifragility.

Current primary evidence spine
The evidence does not establish a market forecast. It constrains what can honestly be called scarce.

SCENARIO DOSSIER / NON-PRESCRIPTIVE

148

ODYLITH / MARKET EVOLUTION SITUATIONAL AWARENESS

EVIDENCE CUTOFF: 5 AUGUST 2026

1. Intelligence and routing inputs are compressing. Stanford's 2025 AI Index reported that inference cost at a GPT-3.5-level
MMLU threshold fell more than 280-fold from November 2022 to October 2024, while open-weight models narrowed some
benchmark gaps with closed models. This weakens scarcity claims based only on access to model portfolios or prompt-level
selection. [Stanford AI Index 2025](https://hai.stanford.edu/assets/files/hai_ai_index_report_2025.pdf)
2. Native routers already exist. AWS Bedrock Intelligent Prompt Routing became generally available in April 2025, and
Microsoft Foundry's model router adds cost/quality profiles, model subsets, and automatic failover. AWS explicitly notes that
its router may be suboptimal for application-specific workloads, which leaves a narrow possible wedge but also defines the
incumbent absorption path. [AWS
announcement](https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2025/04/amazon-bedrock-in…) [AWS router
documentation](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/prompt-routing.html) [Microsoft router
releases](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/foundry/foundry-models/whats…)
3. Agent infrastructure is bundling quickly. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore documents runtime, browser, gateway, identity, policy,
observability, evaluations, memory, and payments in one managed surface. Google Vertex AI Agent Engine documents
sessions, memory, evaluation, observability, security, and managed runtime. This is direct evidence that point products face
suite compression. [Amazon AgentCore
overview](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/what-i…) [Google Agent Engine
overview](https://cloud.google.com/vertex-ai/generative-ai/docs/reasoning-engi…)
4. Open standards are reducing proprietary protocol scarcity. The Linux Foundation launched A2A in June 2025 and the
Agentic AI Foundation in December 2025 with MCP and AGENTS.md among its founding projects. NIST launched an AI
Agent Standards Initiative in February 2026 focused on interoperability, security, identity, and open protocols.
OpenTelemetry 1.43 defines a common semantic-convention layer, with generative-AI conventions maintained in the GenAI
repository. [Linux Foundation A2A](https://www.linuxfoundation.org/press/linux-foundation-launches-the-…) [Linux
Foundation AAIF](https://www.linuxfoundation.org/press/linux-foundation-announces-the…) [NIST
initiative](https://www.nist.gov/artificial-intelligence/ai-agent-standards-init…) [OpenTelemetry
conventions](https://opentelemetry.io/docs/specs/semconv/)
5. Identity and authorization incumbents are extending into agents. Microsoft Entra Agent ID manages agent identities,
sponsors, lifecycle, conditional access, and governance. The OpenID Foundation approved Authorization API 1.0 in January
2026, standardizing communication between policy enforcement and decision points. NIST is separately applying identity
standards and best practices to agent identification, authorization, auditing, non-repudiation, and prompt-injection controls.
[Microsoft Entra Agent ID](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/entra/agent-id/) [OpenID Authorization API
1.0](https://openid.net/specs/authorization-api-1_0.html) [NIST concept paper
notice](https://www.nist.gov/news-events/news/2026/02/new-concept-paper-iden…)
6. Cryptographic provenance does not establish truth. C2PA 2.4 says valid provenance does not decide whether assertions are
good, bad, true, or false; it binds claims to assets and signers. W3C Verifiable Credentials 2.0 standardizes tamper-evident,
machine-verifiable claims but still depends on issuer authority and verifier policy. IETF RATS likewise separates evidence
appraisal by a verifier from the relying party's trust decision. [C2PA 2.4 harms
model](https://spec.c2pa.org/specifications/specifications/2.4/security/Har…) [W3C VC
2.0](https://www.w3.org/TR/vc-data-model-2.0/) [IETF RFC 9334](https://www.ietf.org/rfc/rfc9334.html)
7. Legal authority can be constitutive rather than technical. Under eIDAS, EU national trusted lists have constitutive effect:
qualified legal status exists only when the provider and service appear on the relevant list. This is evidence that legal
recognition and supervised status can create scarcity that an ordinary software signature cannot. [EU trusted
lists](https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/policies/eu-trusted-lists)
8. Regulation creates demand but also changes timing and form. The EU AI Act transparency rules began applying on August
2, 2026, while the July 2026 AI Omnibus moved high-risk rules to December 2, 2027 and product-embedded rules to August
2, 2028. NIST AI 800-4 identifies fragmented logging, immature information sharing, drift detection, and missing trusted
monitoring methods as current barriers. Compliance demand is real, but exact deadlines and harmonized standards can
move. [EU AI Act timeline](https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/policies/regulatory-framewo…) [NIST AI
800-4](https://www.nist.gov/publications/challenges-monitoring-deployed-ai-…)
9. Portability policy can erode lock-in. The EU Data Act requires removal of obstacles to switching and multi-service use, and
eliminates switching and data-egress charges from January 12, 2027. Portability may gain adoption while losing its ability to
command monopoly rents. [EU Data Act explainer](https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/factpages/data-act-explained)

SCENARIO DOSSIER / NON-PRESCRIPTIVE

149

ODYLITH / MARKET EVOLUTION SITUATIONAL AWARENESS

EVIDENCE CUTOFF: 5 AUGUST 2026

10. Payment networks and platforms are claiming agent settlement and intent. Visa released Trusted Agent Protocol in October
2025; Mastercard launched Agent Pay in April 2025; Google donated AP2 to the FIDO Alliance in April 2026; AgentCore now
documents managed agent payments. These actors already own network rules, tokenization, merchant relationships, fraud
systems, or wallets. [Visa Trusted Agent Protocol](https://usa.visa.com/about-visa/newsroom/press-releases.releaseId.21…)
[Mastercard Agent Pay](https://investor.mastercard.com/investor-news/investor-news-details/…) [Google AP2
donation](https://blog.google/products-and-platforms/platforms/google-pay/agen…)
11. Distribution incumbents are also moving. Salesforce launched AgentExchange with more than 200 partners, while AWS
Marketplace agent mode performs conversational discovery, comparison, evaluation, and purchase-proposal work over its
catalog. An independent marketplace therefore needs a source of trust or supply unavailable to these embedded channels.
[Salesforce AgentExchange](https://www.salesforce.com/news/press-releases/2025/03/04/agentexcha…) [AWS
Marketplace agent mode](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/marketplace/latest/buyerguide/agent-mode…)
12. Insurance is not an empty field. Munich Re says it has written AI insurance since its first anti-fraud model policy in 2018 and
now covers contractual liability, financial loss, and legal liability. The NAIC model bulletin and 2025-2026 evaluation work
reinforce that insurers face governance and examination obligations. Any outcome-underwriting thesis confronts incumbents
with capital, licenses, claims operations, and loss data. [Munich Re
aiSure](https://www.munichre.com/en/solutions/for-industry-clients/insure-ai…) [NAIC AI
topic](https://content.naic.org/insurance-topics/artificial-intelligence)
13. Reputation systems face regulatory and adversarial costs. The FTC's final rule bars fake reviews, compensated sentiment,
controlled sites misrepresented as independent, and other manipulation. AI-generated reputation is not automatically an
asset; it can be a liability-bearing attack surface. [FTC final
rule](https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/news/press-releases/2024/08/federal-…)

Existential kill tree
The root claim dies if all paths to durable scarcity close. The branches are conjunctive at the root in the sense that one surviving
control point could still support some economic position, but each individual opportunity must survive its own branch.
`text ROOT: No durable independent position exists around AI trust infrastructure

+-- A. Intelligence coordination is commoditized
+-- model quality differences become too small or transient to monetize
+-- provider-native routers absorb cost/quality/failover selection
+-- customer-specific tuning becomes a feature of the system of record
`-- protocol adapters converge on open interfaces

+-- B. No independent evidence control point forms
+-- telemetry remains customer-owned in cloud data planes
+-- cryptographic provenance authenticates origin but not correctness
+-- buyers accept platform attestations or internal audit
`-- legal recognition belongs to accredited or statutory actors

+-- C. Identity, authorization, and settlement stay with incumbents

EVIDENCE CUTOFF: 5 AUGUST 2026

+-- IAM vendors extend service principals into agent identities
+-- policy interfaces standardize and execution stays near gateways
+-- payment networks bind intent, identity, fraud, and settlement
`-- cloud platforms bundle wallets, budgets, and observability

+-- D. Governance demand does not create proprietary rents
+-- standards become checklists implemented by suites and consultancies
+-- compliance deadlines move or narrow
+-- customer-specific evidence cannot be pooled
`-- switching rules make portability expected and low-margin

+-- E. Reputation and procurement fail to reach credible neutrality
+-- cold start prevents representative outcome data
+-- vendors game metrics and selection
+-- embedded marketplaces own buyer workflow and distribution
`-- liability for rankings exceeds fee capture

+-- F. Underwriting cannot clear capital and causality tests
+-- correlated model/provider failures defeat diversification
+-- outcomes cannot be attributed to a bounded insured mechanism
+-- regulated carriers retain risk while software becomes a vendor
`-- adverse selection concentrates unobservable risks

`-- G. Advanced intelligence removes the remaining wedges
+-- reliable systems self-route, self-verify, and self-document
+-- dominant providers internalize all control-plane functions
+-- open superintelligence drives marginal software value toward zero
`-- sovereign systems prohibit cross-boundary neutral intermediaries

`
Revival requires breaking at least one terminal assumption with retained scarcity. A technical feature that merely remains useful
does not break the tree.

on internal controls or
established labs.
Confounder: certification
marketing without legal
recognition.

Accreditation scopes and
procurement rules explicitly
recognize AI evaluators.

No authority grants
standing, or incumbents
extend existing scopes first.

Kills independent assurance
scarcity; leaves software
supplied to labs.

and measurable.

Confounder: low early

Stable exposure units,

across cohorts.

Switching is common,

Antifragility mechanism audit
The matrix distinguishes the baseline property from the narrower route to positive convexity. "Conditional AF" means the
mechanism can gain from the named shock only inside the stated boundary.

The surviving mechanisms are mostly not AF. Independent custody, accreditation, balance-sheet capital, and off-host
authorization can be durable because they separate duties or carry legal consequences, yet they normally resist shocks rather
than gain from them. The most credible positive-convexity candidates are incident replay, reusable control mappings, repeated
switching maps, and actuarial learning. Each fails when data rights disappear, labels are disputed, events correlate, native suites
retain the observations, or marginal maintenance scales with shock diversity.

Opportunity kill and revival cards
These cards cover functions, not proposed companies. Revenue forms describe possible economic transfer, not market size or
desirability.

C01. Prompt-level model routing
Conditions: persistent cost/quality spread and enough volume to repay evaluation overhead. Buyer: application platform or
inference team. Scarcity: workload labels and routing policy. Revenue: usage fee or savings share. Dependencies: model APIs,
stable evals, telemetry rights. Incumbents: AWS Bedrock, Microsoft Foundry, model gateways. Capital/regulation: low capital;
ordinary data and security duties. Reversibility/decay: highly reversible, but option value decays as native routers and
fixed-quality prices improve. AF class: resistance to provider variance; no inherent AF. Death: native routing meets buyer
tolerances and passes savings through. Revival: application-specific outcomes remain invisible to providers and produce
transferable routing lift.

SCENARIO DOSSIER / NON-PRESCRIPTIVE

156

ODYLITH / MARKET EVOLUTION SITUATIONAL AWARENESS

EVIDENCE CUTOFF: 5 AUGUST 2026

C02. Verification-aware workflow admission
Conditions: consequential actions can be classified before execution and failure costs exceed latency. Buyer: owners of
production agents, security, or operations. Scarcity: outcome-linked admission rules rather than model selection. Revenue:
workflow subscription or action fee. Dependencies: tool semantics, policy enforcement, replay, human escalation. Incumbents:
agent platforms, API gateways, security suites. Capital/regulation: moderate liability; higher in regulated actions.
Reversibility/decay: policies are reversible and shadow-testable; value decays if reliable models eliminate pre-action uncertainty.
AF class: conditional AF through counterexamples and shadow decisions. Death: admission adds delay without reducing loss.
Revival: failures remain jagged and reusable rules outperform model confidence.

C03. Post-deployment evaluation and monitoring
Conditions: drift and unexpected behavior remain material in live context. Buyer: AI operations, risk, audit. Scarcity:
production-grounded evaluators with outcome labels. Revenue: monitored workload, evaluated span, or annual control
subscription. Dependencies: telemetry access, representative sampling, response rights. Incumbents: AgentCore Evaluations,
Google evaluation, observability vendors. Capital/regulation: low financial capital; confidentiality and audit liability.
Reversibility/decay: easy to pilot; option expires as native evaluation closes application gaps. AF class: conditional AF if failures
improve reusable evaluators. Death: customers accept suite metrics or cannot label outcomes. Revival: NIST-style monitoring
gaps persist across platforms and independent methods gain recognized standing.

C04. Conflict-separated evidence ledger
Conditions: a dispute requires records outside the actor being judged. Buyer: legal, audit, regulated operator, insurer. Scarcity:
custody, timestamps, retention, and chain of control, not blockchain mechanics. Revenue: retention subscription, evidence
package, or dispute event. Dependencies: admissibility, identity binding, export from execution systems. Incumbents: cloud logs,
e-discovery, trust-service providers. Capital/regulation: security, privacy, records-management duties. Reversibility/decay:
custody is hard to unwind after reliance; entry option decays when contracts standardize on incumbents. AF class: bargaining
power and resilience, not AF. Death: ordinary customer-owned logs receive equal evidentiary treatment. Revival: contracts,
courts, or carriers require independent custody.

C05. Content and execution provenance
Conditions: buyers care who produced or altered an artifact and under what process. Buyer: media, enterprise governance,
procurement. Scarcity: issuer trust and capture integration. Revenue: signing, verification, credential, or compliance fees.
Dependencies: C2PA/VC compatibility, key lifecycle, trusted issuers. Incumbents: certificate authorities, platforms, content tools.
Capital/regulation: identity and misrepresentation liability. Reversibility/decay: open standards reduce technical lock-in; issuer
reputation decays after compromise. AF class: resistance to tampering, not truth and not AF. Death: provenance has no effect on
reliance or liability. Revival: accepted issuer status or qualified legal effect attaches to specific assertions.

C06. Agent identity lifecycle registry
Conditions: organizations need sponsors, ownership, birth/death, delegation, and inventory beyond workload IAM. Buyer: IAM
and security teams. Scarcity: cross-domain lifecycle evidence. Revenue: managed identity or governed-agent fee.
Dependencies: IAM integrations, directories, token standards. Incumbents: Microsoft Entra, cloud IAM, identity governance
vendors. Capital/regulation: identity security and privacy duties. Reversibility/decay: deployable in stages; option decays quickly
as suites normalize agent objects. AF class: resilience and governance, not AF. Death: agent identity remains a service principal
with metadata. Revival: heterogeneous agents cross domains and no system of record can represent sponsor and delegation
chains.

SCENARIO DOSSIER / NON-PRESCRIPTIVE

157

ODYLITH / MARKET EVOLUTION SITUATIONAL AWARENESS

EVIDENCE CUTOFF: 5 AUGUST 2026

C07. External policy decision and enforcement
Conditions: the agent host cannot be trusted to authorize itself. Buyer: security architecture and regulated operators. Scarcity:
tamper-separated decisioning near consequential actions. Revenue: authorization decision volume or enterprise license.
Dependencies: reliable identity, policy semantics, gateway control. Incumbents: AgentCore Policy, Entra, OPA ecosystems, API
gateways, AuthZEN implementers. Capital/regulation: moderate security liability. Reversibility/decay: policy engines are
replaceable; standards lower switching cost and proprietary value. AF class: resistance; conditional AF through bounded attack
learning. Death: native gateways pass equivalent red-team and audit tests. Revival: cross-platform separation of duties becomes
mandatory.

C08. Delegated-intent and mandate receipts
Conditions: an agent acts for a person or organization and later intent can be disputed. Buyer: merchants, financial institutions,
enterprise approvers. Scarcity: accepted mandate semantics and liability allocation. Revenue: receipt, verification, or dispute fee.
Dependencies: identity, authorization, nonces, revocation, payment rails. Incumbents: Visa, Mastercard, Google/FIDO, wallets.
Capital/regulation: payments, identity, consumer-protection exposure. Reversibility/decay: protocol integration is reversible;
network acceptance windows close quickly. AF class: bargaining power, with conditional learning from disputes. Death:
incumbent network receipts become exclusive trust roots. Revival: cross-network mandates need a neutral verifier accepted by
multiple schemes.

C09. Agent payment orchestration
Conditions: agents buy APIs, content, or goods within hard budgets. Buyer: agent developers and finance operations. Scarcity:
policy-bound execution across rails. Revenue: transaction or orchestration fee. Dependencies: wallets, payment processors,
identity, refunds, spend controls. Incumbents: AgentCore payments, Stripe-related rails, card networks, wallet providers.
Capital/regulation: potentially high if touching funds; lower as an orchestration-only vendor. Reversibility/decay: reversible
integrations; option decays as platforms bundle microtransactions. AF class: resistance through role separation, not AF. Death:
managed platforms expose adequate budgets, audit, and rail choice. Revival: multi-cloud settlement and enterprise
separation-of-duties remain unsolved.

C10. Settlement and reconciliation evidence
Conditions: machine-speed transactions create disputes across authorization, delivery, and payment. Buyer: merchant finance,
marketplaces, payment operations. Scarcity: joined evidence across rails and execution events. Revenue: reconciled transaction
or recovered-loss share. Dependencies: payment data rights, delivery attestations, standardized identifiers. Incumbents:
processors, ERP, payment networks. Capital/regulation: data-security and possibly payments regulation. Reversibility/decay:
narrow proof can start read-only; value decays if networks own end-to-end telemetry. AF class: resilience; conditional AF if
disputes refine reusable matching. Death: one rail provides authoritative joined records. Revival: fragmented rails and machine
services create unresolved multi-party causality.

C11. Outcome-based reputation graph
Conditions: verified outcomes predict future performance after task-mix adjustment. Buyer: procurement, marketplaces, insurers.
Scarcity: representative outcome labels and manipulation resistance. Revenue: data license, diligence fee, or ranking access.
Dependencies: evidence rights, identity, statistical adjustment, appeals. Incumbents: embedded marketplaces, review systems,
analyst firms. Capital/regulation: defamation, fake-review, competition, and data liability. Reversibility/decay: scores can be
withdrawn, but reputational harm is not easily reversible; decay is rapid under gaming. AF class: conditional AF only when
attacks improve defenses faster than trust erodes. Death: out-of-sample prediction fails or cheap Sybils move rank. Revival:
legally sourced verified outcomes become portable across procurement channels.

ODYLITH / MARKET EVOLUTION SITUATIONAL AWARENESS

EVIDENCE CUTOFF: 5 AUGUST 2026

C12. Agent/component marketplace
Conditions: fragmented supply, repeatable packaging, and buyer demand for external discovery. Buyer: developers and
enterprise procurement. Scarcity: distribution, trust, or exclusive supply. Revenue: take rate, listing, or enterprise access.
Dependencies: deployment compatibility, review, support, billing. Incumbents: Salesforce AgentExchange, AWS Marketplace,
cloud catalogs. Capital/regulation: moderate platform and representation liability. Reversibility/decay: listings are reversible;
cold-start option expires as embedded marketplaces consolidate. AF class: possible network effects, not AF. Death: incumbents
own both workflow and catalog. Revival: cross-platform components require neutral certification or buyers reject
vendor-controlled ranking.

C13. Procurement evidence exchange
Conditions: buyers repeatedly request the same controls, benchmarks, and attestations from many suppliers. Buyer: vendor risk
and sourcing. Scarcity: accepted evidence schemas and verified issuers. Revenue: supplier subscription, buyer seat, or verified
package. Dependencies: procurement systems, standards, credential issuers. Incumbents: GRC suites, cloud marketplaces,
audit firms. Capital/regulation: confidentiality and reliance liability. Reversibility/decay: data exchange is reversible; schema lead
decays as standards mature. AF class: bargaining power; conditional AF if each supplier increases reusable comparison quality.
Death: evidence remains bespoke or embedded channels satisfy it. Revival: RFPs require portable machine-verifiable evidence.

C14. Sovereignty and residency policy broker
Conditions: models, data, and actions face incompatible jurisdictional rules. Buyer: global enterprise compliance and platform
teams. Scarcity: executable location/purpose policy and current legal mappings. Revenue: region/workload subscription or
compliance service. Dependencies: cloud regions, legal interpretation, data lineage. Incumbents: cloud policy suites,
consultancies, sovereign clouds. Capital/regulation: high advice and misconfiguration liability. Reversibility/decay: rules can be
staged; mappings expire with legal change. AF class: conditional AF only if new rules reuse a common control graph. Death:
marginal jurisdiction work remains bespoke. Revival: fragmentation persists but common control primitives capture most
variance.

C15. Context and memory portability
Conditions: state affects outcomes and provider exit requires semantic continuity. Buyer: application owners and data
governance. Scarcity: portable meaning, permissions, provenance, and deletion state. Revenue: managed memory, migration, or
export subscription. Dependencies: schemas, embeddings, application semantics, privacy rights. Incumbents: Google Memory
Bank, agent frameworks, databases. Capital/regulation: privacy and retention exposure. Reversibility/decay: export is reversible;
option decays as open formats and Data Act switching normalize portability. AF class: bargaining power and resilience;
conditional AF through repeated migrations. Death: transcripts suffice or state is cheap to rebuild. Revival: verified migration
preserves outcome quality across materially different runtimes.

C16. Independent benchmark and calibration lab
Conditions: buyers distrust vendor benchmarks and decisions require task-specific calibration. Buyer: procurement, regulators,
insurers. Scarcity: representative test access, methods, and trusted independence. Revenue: evaluation project, subscription, or
certification. Dependencies: ground truth, secure model access, anti-cheating controls. Incumbents: internal labs, NIST/CAISI,
established test laboratories, consultancies. Capital/regulation: professional liability; accreditation burden if claims carry authority.
Reversibility/decay: methods can be piloted; option expires if benchmarks saturate or become native. AF class: conditional AF
through counterexamples, but evaluation gaming is an opposite force. Death: tests do not predict deployment outcomes. Revival:
accredited methods and field monitoring close that gap.

SCENARIO DOSSIER / NON-PRESCRIPTIVE

ODYLITH / MARKET EVOLUTION SITUATIONAL AWARENESS

EVIDENCE CUTOFF: 5 AUGUST 2026

C17. Incident and adversarial exchange
Conditions: participants can share sanitized failures and attacks without creating intolerable exposure. Buyer: security, insurers,
sector consortia. Scarcity: trusted membership, normalized incidents, and safe-actionable sharing. Revenue: membership or
sector sponsorship. Dependencies: legal safe harbors, privacy, taxonomy, analyst validation. Incumbents: ISACs, security
vendors, cloud threat networks. Capital/regulation: breach, confidentiality, antitrust, and disclosure risk. Reversibility/decay:
reversible participation; value decays if sharing rights are narrow. AF class: strongest conditional AF candidate if attacks improve
shared defenses faster than attacker learning. Death: underreporting, poisoned data, or correlated loss. Revival: sector-specific
safe harbors and measurable recurrence reduction.

C18. Compliance control mapping and evidence automation
Conditions: multiple frameworks share controls and evidence can be collected automatically. Buyer: compliance and internal
audit. Scarcity: authoritative mappings plus execution evidence. Revenue: enterprise license, control, or audit cycle.
Dependencies: standards, policy systems, legal review. Incumbents: GRC suites, consultancies, cloud compliance products.
Capital/regulation: advice and audit-reliance liability. Reversibility/decay: modular and reversible; mappings age with rules and
harmonized standards. AF class: conditional AF through graph reuse. Death: legal change requires linear manual remapping or
suites bundle equivalent mappings. Revival: recognized crosswalks reduce marginal audit cost across platforms.

C19. Accredited AI conformity assessment
Conditions: law or procurement recognizes independent assessment within a defined scope. Buyer: regulated providers,
deployers, authorities. Scarcity: accreditation, qualified staff, methods, and liability-bearing signature. Revenue: assessment,
surveillance, and renewal. Dependencies: accreditation bodies, standards, jurisdictional recognition. Incumbents: established
laboratories, certification and audit firms. Capital/regulation: high professional, accreditation, and quality-system burden.
Reversibility/decay: accreditation is slow and partly sunk; option expires if established labs occupy scopes first. AF class:
bargaining power, not AF; repeated findings may improve methods. Death: self-assessment remains sufficient. Revival:
conformity or procurement explicitly requires recognized third-party evidence.

C20. AI performance warranty analytics
Conditions: a bounded metric, observable exposure, and causal trigger support a warranty without taking primary risk. Buyer: AI
vendor, adopter, insurer. Scarcity: technical diligence and trigger design. Revenue: analytics, monitoring, or program fee.
Dependencies: telemetry, contracts, carrier capacity, claims data. Incumbents: Munich Re and other carriers, brokers, actuarial
firms. Capital/regulation: moderate if no risk retained; high misrepresentation liability. Reversibility/decay: pilotable by narrow use
case; option decays as carriers internalize analytics. AF class: conditional learning from claims, not balance-sheet AF. Death:
triggers invite gaming or carrier retains all useful data. Revival: carriers need independent technical monitoring across
heterogeneous stacks.

C21. Primary outcome insurance
Conditions: losses are measurable, diversified, priceable, and within licensed appetite. Buyer: AI vendor or enterprise adopter.
Scarcity: capital, license, distribution, underwriting data, claims capability. Revenue: premium and investment income, offset by
losses and expenses. Dependencies: reinsurance, reserving, actuarial credibility, policy wording. Incumbents: Munich Re and
established insurance markets. Capital/regulation: maximal among the cards. Reversibility/decay: costly and slow to enter or exit;
adverse-selection option decays as incumbents learn. AF class: risk pooling is resistance; only claims learning is conditionally
AF. Death: common-model correlation or causality ambiguity defeats pricing. Revival: narrowly bounded independent risks and
durable access to underwriting economics.

C22. Claims evidence and technical adjudication
Conditions: disputes require reconstruction of agent decisions, controls, and outcomes. Buyer: carriers, insureds, counsel,
regulators. Scarcity: conflict-separated evidence, technical expertise, repeatable causality methods. Revenue: case fee, retainer,
or claims-platform license. Dependencies: custody, policy access, expert standards, legal process. Incumbents: forensic firms,
carriers, e-discovery, expert witnesses. Capital/regulation: professional liability and confidentiality, but less risk capital than
insurance. Reversibility/decay: service can begin case-by-case; option decays if carriers build internal teams. AF class:
conditional AF if adjudicated cases create reusable causal patterns with lawful rights. Death: evidence cannot resolve causality or
incumbents retain it exclusively. Revival: heterogeneous stacks make independent reconstruction necessary.

Real-options classification and decay
Classification is descriptive, not an instruction to exercise. Every option needs an owner, paid premium, trigger, expiry, and
abandonment condition. "Keep everything open" is not an option because carrying cost and attention are unbounded.

Cross-examination of the best survivors
• Incident replay looks strongest in a fragmented, failure-rich world. Opposite world: reliable systems make incidents rare, while
the remaining events are novel and correlated. The corpus then loses breadth exactly as tail severity rises. Survival does not
establish convexity.
• Reusable compliance mapping benefits from regulatory variation. Opposite world: harmonized standards erase mappings, or
sovereign rules require local legal judgment. One world removes demand; the other makes marginal cost linear.
• Independent custody benefits from conflicts and disputes. Opposite world: customer-controlled cloud logs gain routine legal
acceptance. Custody remains technically sound but loses bargaining power. Its revival requires a reliance rule, not more
cryptography.
• Claims learning appears to compound. Opposite world: the same frontier provider sits beneath many insureds, so one update
creates portfolio-wide loss. More exposure produces more data only after consuming capital; this is negative convexity unless
limits, exclusions, and reinsurance cap the common shock.

SI does not automatically increase demand for every trust layer. In the first three worlds it destroys most technical intermediation.
In the last two it may increase control demand while simultaneously making neutrality, data pooling, and insurance less viable.

Post-hoc collision with the supplied deck
This analysis was derived before reviewing /Users/freedom/Downloads/Odylith AI.pdf. The deck's adaptive inference,
verification, collective learning, and outcome-underwriting layers map to C01-C03, M04/M11, and C20-C21. Its public benchmark
is evidence for a repo-grounded operating policy and write-admission behavior, not isolated proof of per-step model allocation,
cross-tenant learning, calibrated underwriting, generalization, or economic scarcity. Its illustrative mature revenue envelope is
arithmetic based on assumed enterprise counts and prices, not demand evidence. The software beachhead is coherent as an
experiment surface, but transfer from code workflows to regulated outcome risk remains an unproven assumption. The deck does
not remove the incumbent, data-rights, causality, correlation, accreditation, or capital kill branches.

Decisions deliberately unmade
No opportunity is ranked. No company boundary, financing path, price, market size, launch sequence, jurisdiction, accreditation
scope, risk-retention level, or partner is selected. No mechanism is treated as a moat merely because it survives a shock. The
unresolved decision gates are the falsifiers in A01-A25 and the exercise/abandonment triggers above.
